@@3319717 <h> How we can end ' himocracy ' and 150 years of sexism in Canada 's election system with a single act <p> Here 's an election-year riddle : What do the democracies of Canada , the U.S. and the U.K. , France and Australia all have in common ? <p> These five countries have archaic voting systems that help ensure women are n't equally represented in political decision making . In short , they 're all sexist . <p> Our winner-take-all " First Past the Post " ( FPTP ) approach to voting suppresses the number of women in office . <p> I call it " himocracy . " Feel free to hashtag the hell out of it . <p> In this patently unfair system , Canadian women live under a political glass ceiling . Only 25 per cent of our federal representatives are women . This puts us at 47th in the world for female representation , behind Germany , Denmark and Switzerland ... oh and Angola , Iraq and Afghanistan . <p> Australia has the same problem with its " alternative voting " ( AV @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ top eight countries in the world for female representation at the highest levels of government use what is called " proportional representation " ( PR ) , versus " winner-take-all " systems in their elections . <p> It 's worse than it looks , because this graph is not exhaustive . Compared with all the countries in the world , Canada , the U.S. the U.K. , Australia and France sink down the chart in terms of representation by women . <p> Bear in mind that the United Nations has stated that for women 's concerns to be heard at the national level , women must make up at least 30 per cent of a nation 's elected representatives . Countries that do n't have this suffer from what the UN calls a " democratic deficit . " <p> That 's us , Canada . <h> A few facts about " winner take all " voting systems <p> There are primarily two kinds of democratic voting systems : winner-take-all and proportional . <p> In Canada , more men than women vote for the Conservatives . Women are more inclined to vote @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Under our winner-take-all system , with just 39 per cent of the vote , a party gains a " false majority " and takes complete power . <p> They can then sweep aside the concerns of the majority , as well as the priorities of women . The majority of women 's voices are silenced . <p> Here are those shocking statistics : <p> The U.K. is 36th in the world for female MPs , with about 29.3 per cent women as of the recent 2015 election . <p> Canada rolls in at 47th with 25 per cent women representatives . <p> Australia is 48th with 24.6 per cent . <p> The U.S. lands at 72nd with 19.4 per cent . <p> What else do the democracies of Canada , the US , the UK , France Australia and the US have in common ? <p> None break the most basic barrier for representation of women in politics -- never mind exceed it . <p> " I rarely hear a woman say she does n't want proportional representation , " says Kelly Carmichael of Fair Vote Canada . " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and often partisan men who harbour dreams of their own fiefdom built on a 39 per cent ' majority . ' ' ' <p> Would men quietly accept an electoral system that reduced their representation to 25 per cent ? <p> I doubt it . <p> Our system was invented in Britain when the world was thought to be flat and women could n't vote . It 's a relic of an era when married women were legal chattels of men -- unable to sue for divorce if they were beaten or raped , and no right to own property , even it if was theirs before marriage . <p> We 're still living in a warped , sexist imitation of a democracy . The consequences are evident in our social policy , in how women are treated in parliaments here and around the world ( France providing the most repellent example in recent memory ) , and in how long it has taken to address daycare affordability or build a proper plan for seniors in this country . And in many other issues . <p> It 's true that there @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has 33 per cent women in her House . But her power to make the kind of lasting changes we all need is hobbled by her false majority . The more she does , the more vulnerable she is to the next false majority from the right-wing . <p> This is the reality of a true PR majority which allows Angela Merkel in Germany to implement austerity while building a renewables-based economy , raising the minimum wage , increasing day care spaces and making post-secondary tuition free at the same time . <p> They all want to overturn our " winner take all " system and a century and a half of " himocracy " in Canada . <p> Switching to PR will give us a fair voting system . It 's an urgent civil rights issue , and it 's moving right to the centre of national politics , fast . <p> All 94 NDP MPs in the Official Opposition supported PR fair voting in a vote last December . So did both the Greens and most independents . And more than half the Liberal caucus -- even though Justin @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Trudeau says he needs to hear from Canadians on proportional representation , but actually Canadians have been loud and clear . We are a fair-minded people , so it 's not surprising that seventy per cent of us support proportional representation and have done for years . Add that to ten separate commissions and studies in Canada since 2004 all calling for PR . <p> While there has been some increase in female participation in recent years , as recently pointed out by former prime minister Kim Campbell , who was our one and only female PM in history , at our current rate of growth it will be generations before women in Canada are equally represented in parliament and legislature . I am not willing to wait . <p> Canadians have agreed that PR solves the problems created by a voting system that stifles the voices of women . <h> Why proportional representation works , and what you can do about it <p> As for why proportional representation , which I 've come to simply call " fair voting , " works to elect more women , it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ list all their candidates . And parties that do n't field women candidates are exposed as sexist . <p> A PR system of fair voting allows the public to make informed choices about how much , or how little , each political party represents the voices of women . <p> There are many other reasons to support a PR fair voting system . Canadians yearn for MP co-operation to meet the major challenges of our time . PR rewards and encourages cooperation amongst MPs . Research has proven that , because PR encourages governing parties to work together with minority parties to achieve their goals , countries with fair voting systems have far more progressive policies on renewable energy , climate change and income equality , among other critical issues . <p> Winner-take-all systems , on the other hand , encourage partisan bickering and the kind of cover-ups and disinformation campaigns with which we have sadly become so familiar in our governments . No wonder the idea of PR strikes fear into the heart of the old-boys clubs in the corporate boardrooms and political back rooms . They will actually have @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that with PR , people simply like their governments more and believe they represent them more fairly . That means they participate more in elections . Makes sense right ? 
@@3319730 <h> David Suzuki : On World Oceans Day , let 's learn to treat the seas better <p> June 8 is World Oceans Day . In Canada , it 's a time to celebrate the rich marine life in three great oceans off the longest coastline of any nation -- trillions of plankton , billions of fish , millions of seabirds , thousands of whales , and myriad other creatures great and small . <p> Yet , we have little to celebrate when it comes to looking after this natural legacy . Although the federal government has committed to protect 10 percent of our oceans by 2020 , it has so far protected just one percent . <p> Canada passed the Oceans Act 18 years ago , with a promise to safeguard our oceans and coastal ecosystems . It took another eight years to prioritize five large ocean management areas : the Beaufort Sea , the Gulf of St. Lawrence , the North Pacific Coast , the Eastern Scotian Shelf , and Placentia Bay/Grand Banks . The process has languished and we 're now 10 years behind schedule @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from climate change , and emerging issues such as ocean acidification and plastics pollution . <p> Marine planning , conservation , and protection have never been more important . Energy development , shipping , fishing , conservation , and ecotourism are bumping up against each other with no clear direction . This lack of coordination is like allowing a city to grow without zoning or planning , with schools built next to highways and industrial areas , residential neighbourhoods developed with no green spaces , and first responders unable to get to those in need . <p> In the absence of federal leadership on marine planning and conservation , other levels of government have stepped in to take the lead -- just as they have with climate change commitments . <p> On B.C. 's Pacific North Coast , marine planning has moved ahead without the federal government , which dropped out of the planning process in 2011 . Even without national representation , the results are encouraging . Eighteen coastal First Nations and the province of B.C. have come up with their own plans for coastal ecosystems . <p> Communities in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ marine environment . With competing claims for ocean use and proposals to dramatically increase shipping traffic for oil and gas exports to Asia , these marine plans chart a course through challenging waters . Good public consultation and planning can help avoid expensive and disruptive conflicts that arise with so many interests competing for marine resources . These plans also provide meaningful engagement for industry and government with First Nations , a requirement highlighted in the Tsilhqot'in First Nations Supreme Court decision last June . <p> B.C. 's coastal communities are leading the way in our country 's ocean planning . I 'm happy to honour my relationship with 12 of those communities by embarking on a tour in June to talk about the many issues coastal communities are facing , including climate change . We can see its effects along the coast : shellfish die-offs , low snow packs affecting salmon runs , rising sea levels , and altered growing seasons . <p> These communities depend on healthy oceans for their cultural , social , and economic needs and are searching for solutions . <p> Ian Mauro , an award-winning @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in Atlantic Canada and from an Inuit perspective , is joining the tour with a new film that features climate change 's impact on West Coast communities . <p> Oceans produce half the oxygen we breathe and absorb a third of carbon dioxide emissions . If that were n't reason enough to protect them , there 's also a strong business case . According to a WWF report , oceans provide an annual economic value of at least $24 trillion . In Canada 's Great Bear Sea , ecosystem services are estimated to provide $101 billion in annual benefits to local communities . Tourists pay to experience a clean , healthy ocean with an abundance of life , and marine recreation is projected to be among the fastest-growing sectors in B.C. 's economy for the next 20 years . <p> Marine wildlife and resources are rapidly declining around the world , and our oceans are changing faster than ever , endangering this economic engine and the security and livelihoods it supports . But our oceans are bigger and longer-lasting than any economic system . Canadians love their oceans and want their @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> This World Oceans Day , let 's honour our coastal waters and all the creatures that depend on them -- including ourselves . <p> Written with contributions from David Suzuki Foundation Communications Specialist Theresa Beer.Learn more at www.davidsuzuki.org.

" <p> The potential for lentils is encouraging , says Kasi Rao , vice-president of the Asia Pacific Foundation of Canada and a member of Saskatchewan Premier Brad Wall 's Asia advisory council . <p> " We 're going to see increasing activity in the coming decades , and particular staples such as lentils are going to see a surge in demand , " he says . <p> " Saskatchewan already accounts for the bulk of India 's imports of lentils , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's only going to grow . " <p> While there are several reasons for this growth potential , at the root is increasing demand . The world is getting more populated , living standards in many parts of the developing world are rising and diets are changing , leading to worldwide demand for foods that were once mostly regional . <p> At the same time there are also considerable hurdles to boosting Canadian food exports -- such as bumps in the road in trade negotiations for deals such as the Trans-Pacific Partnership ( TPP ) . <p> The 12-nation TPP , still being negotiated , would affect much more than agriculture . It has become a tough issue for Canada because of U.S. objections to the supply management system that protects our beef and poultry farmers . <p> Canada might be sidelined from this agreement if it maintains supply management , a politically sensitive domestic issue , but one that annoys many of Canada 's trading partners . <p> U.S. President Barack Obama 's administration , which is championing the TPP and facing stiff resistance from some fellow Democrats , has @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ negotiations if it wo n't budge on supply management . <p> This does n't change the potential for Canada to continue to grab a larger share of the food export market for products that are not supply-managed . Canada and Saskatchewan are already the world 's leading supplier of pulses such as lentils and peas , Mr. Potts says . <p> " Canada 's agri-food sector has the potential to become a growth engine for the entire economy , " he wrote in Policy Options magazine . <p> Between 2003 and 2013 , Canada 's trade in agricultural products grew by more than 80 per cent , according to Statistics Canada and the Conference Board of Canada . This growth was second only to the growth in financial services and insurance and one of the top five fastest-growing export sectors . <p> The prospects are good for maintaining and expanding this growth . The world 's population is expected to increase to more than nine billion by 2050 ( more than two billion more than at the beginning of this decade ) and to feed everyone , food production will have @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , author and former CIBC chief economist , argues that Canada 's food production could become even more important to world markets as climate change brings longer growing seasons . <p> " The Prairies , once hailed as the breadbasket of the world , could find that description gain renewed currency in the years to come , " Mr. Rubin posted in his May 12 , 2015 blog . " With climate change set to bring about profound changes to global food production , Canada may come to find itself in something of an unforeseen sweet spot . " <p> Mr. Rao says that , in addition to the major national markets for our food exports , Canadian producers should look beyond the big countries where we traditionally seek markets . <p> " It 's obvious that any global trade and investment strategy should include India , China and Japan . @@3322788 <h> Concerned whale watchers and scientists have bee keeping a keen eye on the endangered southern resident population , which had not had a successful birth in three years . <p> This orca was spotted swimming in March with other members of the J-pod . But in December , tragedy struck when a pregnant female washed up along the shore of Comox , B.C. <p> The mother from J-pod could not deliver her fetus from her uterus , and both died , dropping the population to a dangerously low 77 . <p> " So not only did we not have the baby coming that we needed , but we also lost a breeding-age female at the same time , " Harris said . " Literally the baby was just pulled out by other members of the pod . " <p> In order to ensure that the mother could survive the traumatic birth , the grandmother cared for the calf for the first few days . <p> " That started the baby boom , " Harris said . Within the span of a few short months , four calves were born to the southern resident population , boosting their total numbers to 81 . <p> It 's still a long way from the 120 whales needed in order to be removed from the endangered list , said Eric Ward , who keeps track of the whale population for NOAA Fisheries , an American governmental organization that monitors the health of the oceans . <p> Replenishing the population will @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and only give birth to one calf at a time . Calves only have a 50/50 chance of survival into adulthood , which means that baby L-121 is not out of the woods just yet . <p> " There 's reasons to be optimistic for this population , and there 's reasons to be concerned , " Ward said . <p> Southern residents prefer to dine on Chinook salmon , as well as some other types of fish , and depleted prey stocks have played a large role in the community 's decline , Ward said . Contaminants from fish that spawn near urban centres may also have contributed . <p> But a record run of Chinook in 2014 may have turned things around , as well as demographic shifts in the population towards more females of reproductive age . <p> " This pulse will continue as long as there 's available prey , " Ward said . <p> What is unclear is how climate change will continue to affect their favourite food -- salmon . Chinook prefer cold oceans , but this winter warm water circulated off the coast could @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ do n't know the implications this giant blob of warm water will have on marine life and the killer whales , " Ward said .

@@3326089 <h> News Local <h> Flood of information comes from festival <p> Students pour water through tubes to determine the better flow rate during the first-ever Great Lake Water Festival at Lake Ontario Park in Kingston . The day was intended to educate the students in the need for better water conservation . ( Michael Lea/The Whig-Standard ) <p> Gathering next to Lake Ontario Thursday morning , a group of public school students discovered the apparent unlimited depths of that lake and the other waterways in the province are , in fact , a finite resource in dire need of protection . <p> The students , about 200 of them from Grade 4 classes in seven schools , were invited to Lake Ontario Park to take part in the first Great Lake Water Festival , a pilot project that , it is hoped , will become an annual event to teach children the importance of water conservation and wise resource management . <p> More than 20 interactive stations were scattered throughout the park dealing with different aspects of water to stimulate students to become stewards of water " and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as a resource , " said Ed Gardner , director of public health programs and services at Kingston , Frontenac , Lennox and Addington Public Health , which helped organize the event . <p> He explained there are already about 25 other such festivals in Ontario " but this is new for our area . " <p> " We are blessed in Ontario , in Canada , with what seems to be unlimited water , but there will be a time in the future that water becomes very rare and a valuable commodity , " Gardner said . <p> " It is starting to get there and it is not going to be clean and pristine and valuable if we do n't treat it better . " <p> Gardner said it is time to start looking more closely at what we are doing to our water sources . <p> " Actually , the time has passed but we are trying to make up for lost time right now . " <p> Although students are taught about environmental issues in school , " there is a niche here that needs to be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ He hoped the festival would develop in the children a personal awareness for the importance of water and foster a respect for the environment . <p> Noting that water covers one-sixth of the province , Gardner said water is essential to our health , environment and economy . <p> " Our civilization depends on the availability of water : water to grow food , water to drink , water to wash with , and water to travel on . All cultures and faiths around the world recognize the sanctity of water , " he said . <p> " It is hoped that by increasing awareness and respect as individuals , all of us will make a commitment to use natural resources wisely . " <p> He hoped the day would get children from urban areas to pass on to their parents such basic water conservation methods as avoiding long showers and not overwatering their lawns or washing their cars in time of drought . <p> " They ca n't just throw things down a manhole in the city and not know where it goes , " Gardner said . <p> He @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ their water comes from and how what goes into the ground can affect it . <p> Grade 4 children were chosen to take part in the festival as good potential ambassadors of water conservation . <p> " They are at a great age where they are not too young to understand the message and yet they are still really lots of fun . They want to get in there and be interactive . " <p> " Children learn by doing and by having them see things and feel things and touch things , especially in this beautiful venue with water as our backdrop . We are hoping the message will seed and grow from there . They will have fun , get wet and learn . Then they will take the learning home as a fun message . " <p> Also involved in the festival were the Cataraqui Region Conservation Authority , the Ministry of Environment and Climate Change , the Ontario Provincial Police , the Royal Bank of Canada , the Cataraqui Indigenous Grandmothers ' Council , the Limestone District School Board , Public Health Ontario , Utilities Kingston and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of interests from the community and cultures that are here today basically celebrating water , " said Geoff Hall , the associate director of the water research centre at Queen 's University , which also took part . <p> He said the various stations were designed to give examples of what happens with water on a daily basis in our lives . The children would learn just how much we depend on it . <p> Rotating through the stations , the students got the chance to try washing clothes using old washboards , write messages on rain barrels , learn about the Great Lakes ecosystem , diagnose waterborne diseases , determine what areas were prone to flooding , and learn about the water cycle from precipitation to evaporation . <p> On the fun side , they ran water relays and pumped water to learn how much work a farmer needs to do to water his cows each day . <p> They decided to start out with 200 students from just one school board to keep things manageable for the first year of the festival , Gardner explained . <p> " We @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in Kingston , Frontenac , Lennox and Addington in the years to come . And it may be a multi-day festival at that point . " <p> In his own address to the children , Michael Adamcryck , regional vice-president with the Royal Bank of Canada , said he hopes in years to come " our water is in the same shape it is in today or better because of what you learned today . "

@@3326254 <h> A force of nature : Naturalist , writer and teacher Drew Monkman 's honorary doctorate from Trent University was well-earned and well-deserved <p> Drew Monkman , middle , marches in the Academic procession before receiving an honorary degree during the morning ceremony of the annual Trent University Convocation on Tuesday June 2 , 2015 Katy Trent University in Peterborough , Ont . Clifford Skarstedt/Peterborough Examiner/Postmedia Network <p> On Tuesday morning this week , Drew Monkman , regular author of this column , received an honorary doctorate in a ceremony at Trent University . The degree was awarded in recognition of the work he has done over the years inspiring generations of Peterborough 's residents to share his love of the natural world . This week is therefore a good time to celebrate all that Drew has achieved to date and to reflect on the impact that one person can have on a community and the young people growing up in it . It is also a good time to explore the sources of support for others wishing to help young people connect with their natural surroundings . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as a French immersion teacher in elementary schools . Throughout his teaching career Drew put connecting his students with the wonders of nature at the centre of his work . He did this by spotting the opportunities available around him . For Drew , a tree in the schoolyard is so much more than just a tree ; it affords opportunities for scientific investigations , mathematical calculations , nature-inspired poetry , art projects and the keeping of diaries to record seasonal changes . Above all he believes in opening children 's eyes to the world around them . On a number of occasions when I have met someone from Peterborough under the age of 40 with an interest in the natural world , I have asked them when their interest began and they have said : " When I was in Grade 5 I had this teacher called Drew Monkman " ? " . How wonderful to have had such an influence ! <p> Drew spent most of his teaching career at Edmison Heights Public School in the north end of Peterborough . For more than 20 years he worked with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ community to transform part of the schoolyard into a naturalized learning space . The haven that has grown up is a place where children can observe , study and enjoy nature . They can identify birds , become friends with a tree , observe bees at work pollenating flowers or just enjoy periods of quiet contemplation . This long-term project has been a great example of how a school , with the support of the community , can transform a barren landscape of tarmac and grass into a vibrant and much loved learning resource and nature sanctuary . <p> One of my former students , Allyson Brown was clearly influenced by her time at Edmison Heights : <p> " Before naturalizing playgrounds became a trend , Mr. Monkman was a champion for having a habitat area on the school grounds and this was well established by the time I attended Edmison Heights in 1990 . Some of my favourite memories of elementary school involved the classes that went outside and utilized the natural area and it 's many ties to the curriculum . What better way to learn about biology than @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the habitat their home , or to find a quiet space for creative writing or an art project ? I recall taking a break from all of the soccer and baseball games happening at recess to be one of Mr. Monkman 's recess volunteers , helping to plant seeds or to maintain the habitat on a few occasions . I loved feeling as though I was in the wilderness , only a few steps from the school and that when Mr. Monkman was around , all questions about ' what type of butterfly just flew by ? ' or ' what 's the name of that flower ? ' would be answered . " <p> Allyson is now director of outdoor education at the Bishop Strachan School in Toronto ; Drew 's influence seems to have paid off . <p> There is growing evidence that when children grow up spending time outdoors immersed in nature , it has a major impact on their values and behaviour as adults . Research shows that today 's environmental leaders and champions almost always had chances to interact with nature when they were young and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ nature . The students at Edmison Heights had both of these influences , thanks to Drew . <p> In addition to his inspirational teaching , Drew has been prolific as an author . His particular passion is phenology : the study of seasonal change . Through this weekly column for The Peterborough Examiner he has , over many years , introduced readers to seasonal changes in their local environment and promoted an understanding of the impact of human activities . In addition to his newspaper column he is also the author of two excellent books : Nature 's Year in the Kawarthas , first published in 2002 and Nature 's Year : Changing Seasons in Central and Eastern Ontario , published in 2012 . Each of these books gives a month-by-month account of what to look out for in the natural world . Drew also maintains a website ( drewmonkman.com ) through which he reaches more people with his posts on local nature sightings , an archive of his past newspaper columns and information on the impact of climate change on seasonal patterns in the Kawarthas . He also makes use @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ served on the board of Camp Kawartha and is currently taking a break from his regular Examiner column and collaborating with the camp director , Jacob Rodenburg , to produce a seasons-based activity guide for children and adults . He is a past president of the Peterborough Field Naturalists , an organization that has just celebrated its 75th anniversary , and for whom he regularly leads field outings . Drew is an expert birder and enjoys participating in special bird monitoring projects . An hour or two spent in the field with Drew is a great way to learn how to identify some of the less familiar species of bird to be found in the area . <p> Like most people who take an interest in the natural world that sustains us , Drew worries about the future . He is an active member of For Our Grandchildren , a group that works to increase public awareness of the threat of climate change . He speaks regularly on this topic and a wide range of others concerning the natural world and our role as its stewards . <p> It is appropriate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that is where he began his post-secondary education studying Biology and Geography , before completing a degree in journalism at Universit ? Laval in Quebec City . He later went on to complete a Master 's degree in Education at the University of Toronto . He has won a number of awards for his writing and environmental advocacy , including induction into the Peterborough Pathway of Fame , the Carl Nunn Media and Conservation Award from Ontario Nature , and the Environmental Excellence Conservationist Award from the Otonabee Region Conservation Authority . <p> We may not all have the expertise and level of dedication that Drew has displayed , but any adult can help to mentor the next generation and to instill in them a respect and love of the natural world . Parents , other family members , youth leaders and teachers can all do their part to open children 's eyes to the wonders in the Peterborough region . There are many sources that you can draw on for ideas , encouragement and support . Drew 's forthcoming book and the two he has already published are a great @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ information to be found on-line . Step Outside ( http : **41;344;TOOLONG ) is a great place for formal and informal educators to get up-to-date information on what is going on in nature locally through the year . Three times a month this website produces a guide that details what is happening to plants , animals and the weather in Ontario and what special things to look out for . The Biodiversity Education and Awareness Network ( BEAN ) has a website ( biodiversityeducation.ca ) where you can find useful Ontario-focused resources produced specifically by BEAN or by their partner organizations . <p> Whilst many of the resources are produced with teachers in mind , others lend themselves to use during a family camping trip or stroll in one of the wonderful natural areas that make Peterborough such a beautiful city and county to live in . <p> The Peterborough Field Naturalists run a Junior Naturalists club aimed at 5-12 year olds who want to connect with nature and meet like-minded kids at their monthly meetings and field outings . Teenagers are welcome to attend events and outings organized by the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the clubs best naturalists are not yet in their 20s ! Find out more at the club 's website peterboroughnature.org or simply attend one of the monthly meetings ( 7:30 p.m. , Peterborough Public Library on the third Wednesday of the month , September - June ) or join a field outing ( see websites for details ) . <p> We are very lucky to live in such a beautiful part of the world . Take the opportunity to get out and appreciate it , notice the seasonal changes and look for ways to nurture a new generation of nature stewards . @@3326309 <h> Blog <h> CaGBC BLOG : Energy Benchmarking Plenary Panel <p> The Canada Green Building Council 's Building Lasting Change conference in downtown Vancouver on June 4 was the setting for a plenary panel discussion about energy benchmarking . <p> Michael Brooks , the CEO of RealPac , was the moderator of the panel , which included : Ross MacWhinney , policy advisor for NYC 's sustainability department , David Ramslie of Integral Group , and Nat Gosman , the director of energy efficiency policy for B.C. 's ministry of energy and mines . <p> MacWhinney said New York City is emerging as a leader in energy benchmarking and transparency policy . In the U.S. , cities have taken the leadership role in energy benchmarking , unlike in Europe where usually national governments take the lead . <p> " You ca n't manage what you do n't measure , " he said , and it leaves building owners , policy makers and the real estate market at a disadvantage . <p> The benchmarking program in New York has allowed policy makers to make decisions they could n't before @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ performing buildings are and how they can bring them up to speed . <p> Energy benchmarking began in New York under mayor Bloomberg , and the goal is to " become the most sustainable city in the world and lead the planet on climate change , " MacWhinney said . <p> Cutting emissions from the building sector is where the opportunity is and is executed through the Greener , Greater Buildings Plan , w which requires mandatory benchmarking . <p> It also provides tracking through annual benchmarking and transparency in building energy use . Benchmarking reports are published and available online and disclosure data is also put out to the public for further analysis . <p> Ramslie continued the discussion by showing work the Integral Group did with energy benchmarking for the City of Toronto and the Province of Ontario . <p> The policy proposal they generated was to have a phased implementation of energy reporting starting with 250,000 sq . ft. buildings in year one , followed by 100,000 sq . ft. in year two and 50,000 sq . ft. buildings in year three . Buildings affected include strip malls @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Once the city or province has the data , they will provide a list of buildings with their energy data , with the list updated on an annual basis . <p> For the tool to be useful , the data has to be high quality , Ramslie said , and must be verified by a licensed professional for the first year of reporting and every five years thereafter . <p> Once the data is reported and disclosed , it needs to be implemented into a conservation and demand management plan , Ramslie said . <p> Gosman said the mission of the ministry is " sustainable energy use and development across all sectors of the economy . " <p> Benchmarking in B.C. currently includes the Pacific Coast Collaborative Action Plan on Climate and Energy , which is a commitment with B.C. , Alaska and Pacific Northwest states to collaborate on energy efficient building benchmarking . <p> On the ground interest is coming from local government agencies such as the Union of B.C. Municipalities and an emerging part of the benchmarking effort is the B.C. Climate Leadership Plan . <p> Potential benchmarking @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and reporting requirements for large buildings , provincial/utility administrative and technical support , and a model benchmarking standard for local governments , along with opt-in benchmarking regulations for local governments , paired with provincial requirements . 
@@3326374 <h> Notley looks to Ontario as government plans shift from coal power <p> Perched on the edge of a turquoise-colored Lake Erie , the Nanticoke , Ont. , coal plant that was once Canada 's largest polluter sits idle , surrounded by rolling hills dotted with wind turbines and solar-panelled barn roofs . <p> A few thousand kilometres west , Alberta 's coal-fired generating stations and oilsands operations spew a third of the country 's carbon emissions , while a solar-power potential similar to northern Spain remains largely untapped . <p> Alberta Premier Rachel Notley now wants to bridge the gap in energy policies between the two Canadian provinces . Notley is leaning on advisers from Ontario and the federal wing of her New Democratic Party to outline a shift in Alberta 's energy policy , according to people familiar with discussions who asked not to be identified because plans are n't public . The shift includes phasing out coal plants and offering subsidies for solar and wind , the people said . <p> " Alberta is increasingly unusual in deriving such a high percentage of its electricity @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the Broadbent Institute , an Ottawa-based policy think tank affiliated with Notley 's party . " There are so many cost-effective alternatives these days . You see the tangible , measurable results of this in the greater Toronto area , where the air is cleaner . ' " <h> Campaign Platform <p> While Notley 's government has yet to present any concrete plans , her campaign platform pledged a coal phase-out , an expansion of cleaner energy , as well as loans to families , farms and small businesses to retrofit buildings to reduce energy use . <p> " Failing to deal with climate change issues creates uncertainty , " Notley told reporters in Calgary on May 27 . " To suggest that we never change anything ever , ever , ever going forward is not particularly responsible , given the worldwide conversation around climate change . " <p> Alberta Environment Minister Shannon Phillips said Tuesday that new climate-change regulations will be in place by the end of this month , and that public consultation on the province 's renewable energy and energy efficiency strategies will be held in coming @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ phase-out beyond a federal goal of shutting or refitting plants after 50 years of operations , as well as raising the $15-per-ton price on carbon for large emitters and expanding it to a broader range of sources , said Ed Whittingham , executive director at the Pembina Institute , a Calgary-based environmental research group . Eliminating emissions from coal plants is the " low-hanging fruit , " he said . <h> Biomass Option <p> Though Alberta is Canada 's main oil and gas producing region , the province relies on coal for almost half its electricity generation . Per capita emissions of 60 metric tons a year are about five times the rate for Ontario . TransAlta Corp . and Capital Power Corp . are among the biggest operators of coal plants in Alberta . <p> The Shepard natural gas-fired power plant in Calgary , a joint venture between Enmax Corp . and Capital Power that started operations last month , was the largest step so far in the province to replace coal with a cleaner source . It has the capacity to meet almost half the city 's electricity demand @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ coal plants , according to Enmax . <p> Alberta 's coal plants could follow in the footsteps of Ontario Power Generation Inc. , which converted two stations near Thunder Bay , in the province 's north , to burn wood . The biomass is considered carbon-neutral because the trees capture the gas when they 're growing . Alberta , like Ontario , has ample swaths of Boreal forest . <h> Conversion Cost <p> " The closer you are to the forests , the cheaper the fuel is going to be , " Mike Martelli , vice president of the provincially-owned generator , said by phone from North Bay . <p> Converting one plant cost as little as $5 million , mainly to change safety equipment , and some Alberta coal generators have already reached out to OPG to learn from its experience , he said , declining to identify them . <p> Ontario 's phase-out , completed last year , and policies to boost the use of renewables resulted in a 19 per cent reduction in emissions since 2005 , according to a report by the Pembina Institute . The province @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ levels by 2030 , the most ambitious proposal among Canadian provinces . <p> " What 's really important is that we take on the next steps , " Ontario Premier Kathleen Wynne said at the Bloomberg Economic Series summit in Toronto on May 21 . " We know that there 's serious work to do . If we get it right we 'll provide opportunities for innovation . " <h> Abundant Sunlight <p> Alberta gets 20 per cent more solar radiation than Ontario , John Gorman , president of the Canada Solar Industries Association , said in an interview from Ottawa . Notley 's first task should be to overhaul rules for small-scale solar and then move to encourage development of utility-scale projects because of the province 's availability of land , he said . <p> Alberta 's transition from coal will be slower than Ontario 's as the western province needs to strike a balance between environmental progress and economic growth , TransAlta CEO Dawn Farrell said in an e-mail . <p> By 2030 , more than half of the province 's coal plants will be retired , will meet @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , gas or coal plants that capture and store carbon , Farrell said . <p> Not everyone is happy with Ontario 's approach . Electricity prices have risen to about 16 cents per kilowatt-hour , from less than 10 cents in 2009 , according to the Ontario Energy Board . And the pace of change has been rapid in places like Haldimand Country , home to the Nanticoke plant , which employed 600 workers before it was shuttered . <h> Culture Shock <p> " They just shut off the switch , " said Bernie Corbett , a councillor and former mayor of the county . " It has been a culture shock for us . " <p> The town of 40,000 about 115 kilometres southwest of Toronto is becoming a retirement community . Schools are closing and it 's struggling to navigate a decline in manufacturing in the province . Closing the coal plant did n't help , Corbett said . <p> Still , opportunities have also arisen . Instead of hundreds of workers clocking in at the plant , renewable-energy developers employ technicians like Duke Tombia , a 34 year-old @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ site about a 25-minute drive along the Lake Erie shore from Nanticoke . <h> Bet on Solar <p> " I 've really made a bet on the solar industry , " said Tombia , who previously worked in the construction industry . The thousands of solar panels in the site generate 100 megawatts of emission-free power when the sun is shining . <p> Alberta has the opportunity to tread a similar path , the Broadbent Institute 's Smith said . <p> " If this were 50 years ago and Alberta did n't have options in terms of other cost-effective forms of electricity generation , it would be a different story , " he said . This is an issue of personal interest to Merkel , who has sometimes been called the Climate Chancellor since she chaired her first G7 in 2007 . <p> What Merkel badly wants from her G7 colleagues are green reforms that Germany can take to the climate summit in Paris in December . But her own government 's record on this issue is spotty , at best . <p> Attempts to create wind and solar power have made Germany a global pioneer in this field . @@3329540 <p> Pas dans ma cour is as alive and well in Quebec as " not in my backyard " is in the rest of Canada . <p> Quebeckers , by a margin of 69 per cent to 25 per cent , understand the reality of climate change : It exists and is caused by human activities . Doing something about climate change is another matter , however , according to findings from a recent survey in Quebec that offers lessons for the rest of Canada . <p> Quebeckers are in favour of expanding renewable energy sources , provided they are not built close to where they live ( with the exception of solar panels and wind turbines ) and that it wo n't cost them more as consumers of energy . Only 11 per cent favour a nuclear plant , while 60 per cent oppose high-tension transmission lines , in a province dependent for more than half its power on hydro . <p> Almost half of Quebeckers favour a project for natural-gas pipelines and drilling for natural gas , but only one-quarter favour such a project near them @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Quebec to New Brunswick take note : By a margin of 54 per cent to 36 per cent , Quebeckers oppose the idea of oil pipelines . <p> These numbers come from a comprehensive survey of Quebeckers ' attitudes toward climate change done by L ? ger Marketing for two Montreal-based think tanks , CIRANO and the Trottier institute . The survey of more than 1,000 residents found the same sort of opposition to paying more for renewable energy that has cropped up elsewhere in Canada ( see Ontario ) . <p> Sixty-one per cent of Quebeckers say they would not pay $50 a month more to support the development of renewable energy sources . Half would oppose any increase at all . Even in a province blessed with abundant hydroelectric power , most Quebeckers would not pay another cent to support more renewables . Renewables are fine , Quebeckers seem to be saying , as long as we consumers do n't have to pay for them . <p> Would the reaction to higher prices be terribly different in other provinces ? Look at the political paddling the Ontario Liberals took @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and flow-through tariffs to subsidize wind ( and solar ) power . The least that can be said is that higher energy prices risk a political backlash , although the government that implemented a carbon tax in British Columbia won re-election . <p> Quebec and Ontario have agreed to join California in a cap-and-trade market for carbon credits . The system is not yet running , and it is impossible to predict how it will work . Politically , cap-and-trade is less risky than a carbon tax because the costs are borne by carbon producers . But , of course , eventually producers will push those higher costs to consumers . Then we shall see the reaction . <p> A carbon tax is off the table in Quebec and across Canada , except in British Columbia . But the L ? ger survey shows what might happen in Quebec -- and the same reaction would likely occur across Canada -- were a tax imposed and the price of gasoline were to hit $2 a litre . <p> Asked how they would react in that event , and given a number of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ car ( 38 per cent ) ; shop closer to home ( 28 per cent ) ; buy an electric car ( 24 per cent ) ; use more public transit ( 19 per cent ) ; car pool ( 18 per cent ) ; drive more slowly ( 17 per cent ) . In other words , most Quebeckers ( though not all ) would respond to the new reality of higher-priced gasoline . Which is exactly what proponents of a carbon tax predict . <p> Getting from widespread resistance to higher prices to implementing a carbon tax , with the eventual adjustments in lifestyle and transportation choices , is a bridge too far for anyone in politics today . Even in British Columbia , the pump price is nowhere near $2 a litre . Cap-and-trade ( and the federal Conservatives ' promised , but never realized , regulations on the oil and gas industries ) seems politically safer . <p> The L ? ger survey illustrates the lack of a serious appreciation of fundamental energy facts in Quebec . The population consumes energy , is surrounded by it , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and could exploit natural gas by fracking and oil by drilling in the St. Lawrence River . <p> Yet , only 11 per cent of Quebeckers say they have a " great " or " very great " understanding of the energy situation in their province . The rest say the issues are too complicated , they do n't have time to consider them or they are not interested .

@@3329686 <h> G7 expectations : Here 's how countries have lived up to their promises ( or not ) from the last 10 summits <p> Since 1975 , the leaders of a small club of the world 's most powerful nations have gathered on an annual basis to hob-nob and hash out global problems . While the G7/G8 meetings often end with feel-good calls to action , the summiteers have often been accused of failing to follow through on their promises . As the G7 prepares to meet in Bavaria this weekend , the Post 's Victor Ferreira checks up on the vows made at the last 10 summits . <h> 2005 -- Gleneagles hotel , U.K. <p> THE SUMMIT <p> The 31st G8 summit was overshadowed when suicide bombers killed 52 people in the London Underground and on a bus . Former U.S. President George W. Bush was also involved in a highly publicized incident when he lost control of his bicycle and struck a British police officer , who ended up on crutches and missed three months of work . <p> THE VOW <p> Leaders agreed to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ $50 billion in global aid -- $25 billion of which would go to Africa -- along with 75,000 peacekeeping troops . The existence of global warming , and its link to humans , was also agreed upon by leaders in a shared statement . <p> THE DELIVERY <p> The Gleneagles summit is widely considered one of the most successful G8 meetings in history because of the various pledges made , but the G8 had a tough time keeping promises . By 2011 , the Guardian reported that only $11 billion of the promised $25 billion had been delivered to Africa . <h> 2006 -- Constantine Palace , Saint-Petersburg , Russia <p> THE SUMMIT <p> Attentions were drawn away from the 32nd summit because of the Israel-Lebanon conflict that broke out during the second day of meetings . Russia President Vladimir Putin decided a focus of the summit would be on global energy security after U.S. Vice President Dick Cheney accused Russia President Vladimir Putin of using energy resources as " tools of intimidation and blackmail . " <p> THE VOW <p> Through the St. Petersburg Plan of Action , member nations @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ energy and increasing transparency , predictability and stability of global energy markets . <p> THE DELIVERY <p> Not much was accomplished at this summit and while member nations negatively responded to the Israel-Lebanon conflict , no action was taken other than providing a list of suggestions for the embroiled parties . <h> 2007 -- Heiligendamm , Germany <p> THE SUMMIT <p> French President Nicholas Sarkozy was the talk of the 33rd G8 summit when he appeared to be drunk at a press conference after a " long meeting " with Russian President Vladimir Putin . Sarkozy said he was simply short of breath . <p> THE VOW <p> The summit focused on global warming as member nations agreed , in a non-binding communiqu ? to halve carbon dioxide emissions by 2050 and to help fund climate-protection projects in developing countries . <p> THE DELIVERY <p> The long horizon means its too early to tell , but achieving consensus on emission reductions has been a tall order . <h> 2008 -- Hokkaido , Japan <p> THE SUMMIT <p> The 34th G8 summit known for being the first conference to also include the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ China , India , Mexico , and South Africa . <p> THE VOW <p> Global warming and aid to Africa were once again central as leaders set a five-year deadline on providing $60 billion to help combat disease in Africa and once again promised to halve global greenhouse gas emissions by 2050 . <p> THE DELIVERY <p> Leaders simply used the occasion to reaffirm past promises and attempt to get back on track with broken ones . <h> 2009 -- L'Aquila , Italy <p> THE SUMMIT <p> World leaders were originally supposed to meet in the city of La Maddalena , but Italy Prime Minister Silvio Berlusconi decided to move the meeting in order to show support for the earthquake stricken region of L'Aquila . <p> THE VOW <p> Leaders reaffirmed their commitment to past agreements on climate change and providing aid to Africa . @@3329740 <p> Friday , June 5 , 2015 , 12:21 PM - Scientists met in Whistler , B.C. recently to better understand how the Arctic is connected to the rest of the world 's climate and weather . <p> This is a meeting of the brightest minds in the field . The Congress of the Canadian Meteorological and Oceanographic Society jointly held with the American Meteorological Society 's Conference on Polar Meteorology and Oceanography . <p> So what to do at a meeting with all of this brain power ? <p> I wanted some questions answered for myself . As a meteorologist , I see the wild swings in daily and weekly weather and have always been skeptical about the direct link between parts of our changing climate and specific events of extreme weather . <p> There 's been a lot of attention recently is the connection between shrinking Arctic sea ice and extreme weather , such as the recent harsh winters in parts of Eastern North America and even Hurricane Sandy . <p> Arctic warming and the loss of Arctic sea ice is a well-documented trend . Despite @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is clear . There is reasonable confidence that we will see a nearly ice-free summertime Arctic ocean in the next 40 years . And this trend is largely the result of human activities : greenhouse gas emissions . <p> Despite the swings from year to year , the overall downward trend is clear . <p> But can we link changes in the Arctic to specific weather events as some research has suggested ? Not likely , says Dr. Elizabeth Barnes , an assistant professor at Colorado State University . <p> Barnes is one of the top young minds in atmospheric science . If you have n't watched her in the video above , go back and watch it . Barnes has examined whether Arctic warming has significantly influenced the mid-latitude jet stream . <p> I like to think of the jet stream as the director in the great atmospheric drama that unfolds around the mid-latitudes of the world on a daily and weekly basis . It 's the ups and downs of the jet stream that dictate the storm track , and in turn the extremes in our weather . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the jet stream pattern tends to overwhelm whatever contribution may come from a warming Arctic . This is still an area of active research , but at the very least we should treat any study that tries to directly link Arctic warming to a given weather event or pattern with extreme caution . <p> We need to think big picture . Even if changes in the Arctic do n't directly impact the severity of a winter in a southern Canadian city , the global changes taking place are serious . <p> People are conditioned to look for immediate cause and effect . It 's understandable to want to tie a storm that just hit us to increased greenhouse gases . Most of us have to be forced to plan for the future . This means that critical global issues such as climate change do n't register in our daily lives . Unfortunately , there is a tendency to use the immediate impacts of weather to scare us into action on climate change . <p> While there is an argument that the ends justify the means , there is danger in this @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it 's primarily human induced gives ammunition to climate change skeptics when that trend reverses the next year . <p> Climate change is hard to grasp . We experience the weather . We do n't experience the climate . A warming climate changes the frequencies of certain types of extremes , but the vast majority of what we experience day to day in southern Canada is due to the natural fluctuations of the jet stream -- the result of a spinning world and the heating of the sun . <p> Human-induced global warming is happening and will continue to happen -- this is settled . Scientists understand how greenhouse gases contribute to warming . What 's more difficult to determine is how the circulation patterns that drive our weather could change in the future . <p> Regardless of this uncertainty , we should care about climate change because of the impacts on global sea-level rise and the massive impact expected on hundreds of millions in vulnerable cities and because of acidification of the oceans and the impact on the food chain . The climate will warm and this will have major @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ win and who will lose are not clear cut . <p> It 's important that we communicate the science of climate and weather as clearly and truthfully as possible . And just because we ca n't link a given weather event to climate change , does n't mean we should throw the baby out with the bathwater .

Their origins might be human error or just accident , but what happens when a perpetrator ( terrorist ) admits responsibility ? The impact is still pretty much the same and that 's what counts for short-term events like this . " <p> Power says lessons learned from the London Olympics show the need for an integrated command structure and co-ordinated communications . <p> " All people involved have to understand the need for a single unified command structure , " he says . <p> With the Pan Am security budget doubling to nearly $250 million , Alan Bell , president of Globe Risk International Inc. , says the key question is not how much organizers are spending , but what they 're buying with their cash . <p> " Have decision-makers considered the full spectrum of threats that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Have all threats been clearly defined and vulnerabilities identified ? " <p> The message for the need to prepare for all types of disasters is finally taking hold , says conference chairman Paul Kovacs , executive director at the Institute for Catastrophic Loss Reduction , <p> " Instead of spending so much time responding and rebuilding after something bad happens , everyone agrees we need to put more effort into being prepared and preventing things from happening in the first place , " says Kovacs . <p> " Disasters are growing in complexity , yet the common sense steps to prevent them remain relatively simple . @@3335670 <p> Canada has joined other Group of Seven leaders in pledging to stop burning fossil fuels by the end of the century , but Canadian officials are playing down the promise as an " aspirational " target and Stephen Harper says it will only be reached through advances in technology . <p> In their end-of-meeting statement , G7 leaders called for an end to fossil-fuel use by the global economy by 2100 as well as cuts to greenhouse-gas emissions by 2050 that lower them as much as 70 per cent from 2010 levels . <p> The G7 statement represents a watered-down goal from what German Chancellor Angela Merkel as host of the ? summit had sought . Ms. Merkel had been pushing for a commitment to a low-carbon economy , or relatively light use of fossil fuels , by 2050 . <p> A Western diplomat said European countries in the G7 went into the meeting looking for stronger language about moving to a global low-carbon economy , and it was Canada , a net exporter of energy , along with Japan , who wanted to push back the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ one diplomat noted , the G7 's final communiqu ? , which calls for decarbonisation of the global economy " over the course of this century , " allows each country to put a different interpretation on whether that would happen nearer to 2050 or 2100 . But the notion of decarbonisation , at least , was agreed upon . <p> Lutz Weischer , the team leader for international climate policy at Germanwatch , a non-government organization that advocates sustainable development , said , ultimately , Canada did n't want to be seen as " a one-country minority . " <p> The Prime Minister 's Office denied that Canada had been trying behind the scene to soften language and commitments on fighting climate change . " There was a consensus and Canada supported that outcome , " PMO spokesman Stephen Lecce said . <p> By having the G7 leaders present a united front on climate , Ms. Merkel did achieve her goal to lay the groundwork for an international agreement on climate , to be discussed in Paris in December . <p> " Mindful of this goal ... we emphasize that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ decarbonisation of the global economy over the course of this century , " the leaders of the U.S. , U.K. , Germany , France , Japan , Italy and Canada said in a communiqu ? . <p> G7 member countries agreed to a goal of limiting the increase in global temperature to less than 2 degrees Celsius above pre-industrial levels . <p> For Mr. Harper , a politician from petroleum-rich Alberta , the pledge comes as a surprise , since it amounts to slapping to an expiry sticker on one of Canada 's major economic drivers , including the oil sands . But these commitments impose no firm obligations on Mr. Harper 's government in the short term and he said results will be achieved through technology , not economic sacrifice . <p> " I do n't think we should fool ourselves . @@3335672 <h> G7 agrees to phase out fossil fuels by end of the century <p> The seven industrial nations that form the so-called G7 agreed Monday to phase out the use of fossil fuels by the end of the century , in an effort they believe would help reduced greenhouse gases . <p> The leaders of the U.S. , Canada , Germany , France , the UK , Japan , and Italy , who are meeting in the German region of Bavaria this week , said they have committed themselves to the need to " decarbonize the global economy in the course of this century . @@3335741 <h> News Archive <h> Making waves : A survey of oceans research at Dal <p> Alana Milner - June 8 , 2015 <p> Dal 's oceans research crosses many different disciplines . ( Provided photo ) <p> The oceans cover approximately 70 per cent of the earth 's surface . Whether or not we know it , we all depend on the ocean . The ocean helps produce the oxygen we breathe , acts as a major transportation route for goods , is a major supplier of food and is a favourite place of outdoor enthusiasts . <p> Even though the ocean plays such a major role in our everyday lives , there is still a lot for us to learn . Over 100 researchers across nine faculties at Dal are working every day to make new discoveries about the ocean . From basic science research to new technologies , Dalhousie 's internationally recognized researchers are creating global solutions to the complex challenges facing our life-sustaining oceans . <p> " Dal has the full package of ocean research , " says Paul Hill , chair of the Department @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in social sciences , basic science and the development of new technologies . Together these areas of expertise allow Dal to deliver great education , great science and inform great policy decisions . " <h> The ocean 's connections <p> The ocean is a complex environment , and its complexities grow as you examine at the role it plays with the rest of the world . As we discover new information about the ocean , we discover connections with the atmosphere , environment and human health . New discoveries also allow researchers to look at other areas -- not only within the ocean , but non-marine environments as well to predict what may change or why things may have changed . <p> Julie LaRoche , a biology professor and Canada Research Chair in Marine Microbial Genomics and Biogeochemistry , is helping identify how changes in the ocean may affect the environment and marine organisms , by studying marine microbiomes . <p> " Microbes are the dominant organisms on the planet , " explains Dr. LaRoche . " They are often the first organisms to adapt when a change it made to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ can identify what may be causing the change , why it 's happening and how it may impact other organisms such as fish populations . " <p> By understanding how marine environments are changing we can work to reduce environmental impacts and ensure species health . <p> The ocean also consists of particles , found in everything from ocean spray to ships , connecting the ocean to our atmosphere . Once in the atmosphere , particles play a key role in our climate affecting the environment and human health . <p> " The atmosphere and ocean are connected in a variety of ways such that progress in understanding either one benefits the other , " says Randall Martin , professor in the Department of Physics and Atmospheric Science and in Department of Chemistry . " By applying a combination of satellite remote sensing , chemical transport modeling , and in situ measurements , we can gain insight into what the future climate may look like and use this information to see how the change may impact local , national and international environments . " <p> The marine ecosystem 's complexities make @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ under pressures from human activities and climate variation . <p> Sara Iverson , professor in Biology and scientific director of the Ocean Tracking Network , studies marine mammals , seabirds and fishes to learn about the impacts of changing oceans on their populations . <p> " Marine ecosystems can be altered by many factors , including climate change and fisheries activities , " explains Dr. Iverson . " As ecosystems evolve , the abundance , distribution , interactions , and survival of marine species also change . " <p> Tracking technology helps Dr. Iverson understand how marine species move through their underwater world , with whom they interact , how they survive , and how they react to environmental change . The results can then be used to devise ways to help protect these species and yet still look to the oceans as a major food source . <p> Paul Bentzen , a professor in the Department of Biology , is developing tools that identify the diverse populations that make up Canada 's fisheries , where they occur , and how much mixing occurs between them . This information allows the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> " By developing novel software tools and improving existing software we can ease analysis of the huge amounts of data involved , and enable management of mixed-stock fisheries and conservation of biodiversity within economically and culturally important fish species , " says Dr. Bentzen . <p> This information will help the fisheries be more productive , stable , and subject to fewer closures . <p> The ocean also connects us to one another : in fact , 90 per cent of world trade is done through shipping . <p> " International navigation , sustainable shipping and trade rely on uniform rules , " explains Aldo Chircop , a professor at the Schulich School of Law and MELAW 's Associate Director . " I explore factors that facilitate or constrain uniform standards and their enforcement in shipping , and how international and Canadian maritime law can be further developed and harmonized . " <p> By identifying issues that call for international and Canadian maritime regulatory reform , we can develop policies needed for safe shipping practices , that will help support the global economy . <h> The power of collaboration <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , institutions and industry . Through collaborations , researchers are providing Nova Scotia and Canada with the ability to innovate , commercialize and impact public policy . <p> One driver for collaboration is the large amount of information being gathered on ocean research from around the world . Advances in acquisition , management and analysis are needed to help understand and manage the diverse range of information . <p> Stan Matwin , a professor in the Faculty of Computer Science and Canada Research Chair in Visual Text Analytics , is applying his big data analysis research to help ocean researchers answer questions concerning complexities of the ocean . <p> One area of research has Dr. Matwin collaborating with researcher Boris Worm 's lab in the Department of Biology . Dr. Matwin is looking at the analysis of ship trajectories by using information from the ships Automatic Identification System ( AIS ) to mine interesting facts , regularities and anomalies . The information can help identify ships travel patterns and whether or not a trawler is fishing . <p> Researchers in Boris Worm 's lab are using the information to see how @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ while industry is using it to develop modelling software to improve transportation . <p> Collaboration also comes from a need for research technology to support the growing areas of ocean research . <p> " In working closely with ocean scientists we can develop the technology they need to expand their research scope and capabilities , " says Zhizhang ( David ) Chen , head of the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering . " Researchers in our Faculty are leading the way in areas of underwater acoustic communications and sensing as well as marine robots . " <p> The research technology can be applied to the real-time monitoring of the ocean environment . This allows for fast extraction of biogeochemistry data for understanding the state of the oceans , operations of search and rescue missions , and discovering of new marine phenomena by allowing researchers to look further and deeper into the oceans than before . <p> Industry is also driving collaboration at Dal . Jon Grant , a professor in the Department of Oceanography and NSERC-Cooke Industrial Research Chair in Sustainable Aquaculture , brings novel approaches to the aquaculture industry @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fish farm should be placed , how it may impact the marine ecosystem and how the industry can be more sustainable in its practices and growth . He also works with collaboratively with other researchers across Dal 's Faculties to help answer questions around the industry . <p> " I was approached by industry with the concern of farmed fish escaping their enclosures and interacting with wild fish , " says Dr. Grant . " I helped them engage Darrel Doman in the Faculty of Engineering to measure stress factors on enclosures and help improve cage design . " <p> By using research to understand more about aquaculture , industry and environmental concerns can be prevented and mitigated for a more sustainable food future . <p> " Our research findings can be applied to understand how aquaculture can fit into the marine ecosystem without impacting other areas of fisheries or the environment , " says Dr. Grant . " It can also be applied to how the industry growth can not only be sustainable , but also a benefit to our economy . Cooke Aquaculture is an excellent partner in these @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' <p> The ocean is a large driver of our economy . It provides transportation , living and non-living resources . To safely and sustainably access and utilize these resources , we need to better understand the processes of the ocean . <p> " Dal is located not only in a province , but a country with fantastic ocean resources that can benefit our economy , " says Dr. Hill . " However Canada 's blue economy underperforms with only 2 per cent of our GDP coming from the ocean sector , where other countries have 4-5 per cent . <p> " We need to expand this area and Dal is poised to help Nova Scotia and Canada do so . Our researchers are making the discoveries that will help us make good decisions for the future as we continue to use the great ocean resource wisely and safely . @@3335765 <h> Minimizing the risks of extreme weather the focus of UQAM gathering of climate experts <p> " In the coming decades , the loss of life associated with the increase in natural disasters will be second only to civil war , " says Philippe Gachon , a professor of hydro-climatology in the department of geography at UQAM.Allen McInnis / Montreal Gazette <p> There 's nothing worse than having to go to summer school on a rainy , miserable Monday . <p> Unless , of course , you 're a climate expert trying to boost your expertise on how to minimize the risks of climate and climate change . <p> For the climatology crowd gathered at the Universit ? du Qu ? bec ? Montr ? al on Monday , the soggy weather here was negligible ; they are far more concerned with hydro-meteorological hazards such as floods , cyclones and drought , and their impact on world economics and health . <p> The 50 meteorologists , scientists , academics and students who gathered at UQAM for the start of a five-day " summer school " brainstorming session are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the 21st century : weather risk management and reducing the impact of extreme weather conditions . <p> United Nations Secretary-General Ban Ki-moon warned in March that the 2015 Global Assessment Report on Disaster Risk Reduction shows that economic losses stemming from disasters are now costing as much as $300 billion annually . <p> " We are playing with fire , " he said in a statement . " There is a very real possibility that disaster risk , fuelled by climate change , will reach a tipping point beyond which the effort and resources necessary to reduce it will exceed the capacity of future generations . " <p> Although the 187 UN member nations adopted a new framework for disaster risk reduction in Japan later in March , the UQAM conference aims to bring together stakeholders and create more efficient decision-making surrounding high-impact weather events , said Jennifer Milton of the meteorological service for Environment Canada , which collaborated on the event with UQAM . <p> " In the coming decades , the loss of life associated with the increase in natural disasters will be second only to civil war @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in the department of geography at UQAM who gave the opening address at Monday 's conference . " This is a very important international issue . Substantial action must be taken by the international community to reduce the risks caused by climate change . " <p> One of the unique missions of the first-time conference is to address the communication issues associated with high-impact weather such as meteorological warning systems , intervention in crisis situations , the challenges of risk communication and communications planning . <p> A good warning system that gives people even 20 minutes notice about a hailstorm could help reduce damages because it gives them time to put their cars in garages , said Bernard Motulsky , who holds a research chair in public relations and communications at UQAM . <p> The day is coming , he said , when cell phones and social media will allow meteorologists to pinpoint who is in harm 's way before the arrival of extreme weather and permit them to send direct warnings . <p> However , until that day , there are still hurdles to overcome . For example , Quebec @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ alerts through television networks and radio stations . <p> A recent test simulation of the system done by the Centre des op ? rations gouvernementales in the Quebec City region had poor results , and some local media reported that many listeners complained the signal created a deafening noise that was incomprehensible . <p> Motulsky said climate , communications and geography specialists will have to work together to address the challenges related to the risk associated with extreme weather . <p> With summer almost here , he said , heat waves are a real concern . " They can have major health impacts , especially on vulnerable or elderly people . But they are n't as obvious to address as a snowstorm . " <p> In the spring of 2011 , Quebecers living along the shores of the Richelieu River found themselves wading through hip-deep waters in what was one of the worst floods in Quebec 's history . Residents of the Richelieu Valley suffered more than $78 million in losses . <p> Unfortunately , flooding is one of the most common hydro-meteorological hazards . In Canada alone , the Disaster @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to more than 210 events since 1970 , with total payments of more than $3.4 billion made to provinces and territories . Of those 210 events , 190 were flood-related . <p> Gachon concurred that there has been an increase in natural disasters in the last couple of decades and that floods are one of the big problems . But snowstorms and heat waves are also important . <p> " This is a summer school on weather risks because there are more and more problems around the world , " said Gachon . " And the impact of these events in terms of economic costs and health , in terms of mortality rates , is huge .
